The Riesz transform on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs in the Heisenberg group Thanks: V. C. was supported by Simons Foundation Collaboration grant 521845. R. Y. was supported by NSF grant 2005609
Vasileios Chousionis, Sean Li, Robert Young

Abstract
. We prove that the Heisenberg Riesz transform is L 2 L_{2} –unbounded on a family of intrinsic Lipschitz graphs in the first Heisenberg group ℍ \mathbb{H} . We construct this family by combining a method from [ NY20 ] with a stopping time argument, and we establish the L 2 L_{2} –unboundedness of the Riesz transform by introducing several new techniques to analyze singular integrals on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs. These include a formula for the Riesz transform in terms of a singular integral on a vertical plane and bounds on the flow of singular integrals that arises from a perturbation of a graph. On the way, we use our construction to show that the strong geometric lemma fails in ℍ \mathbb{H} for all exponents in [ 2 , 4 ) [2,4) . Our results are in stark contrast to two fundamental results in Euclidean harmonic analysis and geometric measure theory: Lipschitz graphs in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} satisfy the strong geometric lemma, and the m m –Riesz transform is L 2 L_{2} –bounded on m m –dimensional Lipschitz graphs in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} for m ∈ ( 0 , n ) m\in(0,n) .



1.
Introduction

Given a Radon measure
\nu
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the
m
–dimensional Riesz transform is formally defined by

T^{m}\nu(x)=\int R_{m}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y),

where
R_{m}(x)=x|x|^{-m-1}
is the
m
–dimensional Riesz kernel. If
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an
m
–dimensional Lipschitz graph and
\nu_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{H}^{m}|_{\Gamma}
is the restriction of the
m
–dimensional Hausdorff measure on
\Gamma
, then

f\mapsto T^{m}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Gamma}]

defines a bounded operator in
L_{2}(\Gamma):=L_{2}(\mathcal{H}^{m}|_{\Gamma})
. This fundamental result was first obtained by Calderon in
[
Cal77
]
for
1
–dimensional Lipschitz graphs in the complex plane with sufficiently small Lipschitz constant. (In this case the
1
–dimensional Riesz kernel
R_{1}
essentially coincides with the Cauchy kernel
k(z)=z^{-1},z\in\mathbb{C}
.) The restriction on the Lipschitz constant was removed a few years later by Coifman, McIntosh and Meyer
[
CMM82
]
. Finally, Coifman, David and Meyer
[
CDM83
]
proved that
T^{m}
is bounded in
L_{2}(\Gamma)
for all
m
–dimensional Lipschitz graphs
\Gamma
by showing that the
m
–dimensional case can be reduced to the
1
–dimensional case via the method of rotations.

The
L_{2}
–boundedness of Riesz transforms on Lipschitz graphs has been pivotal for the research program which started in the early 80s with the aim of relating the analytic behavior of singular integrals on subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the geometric structure of these sets. In particular, David and Semmes
[
DS93
,
DS91
]
developed the theory of uniform rectifiability hoping to characterize the
m
–Ahlfors regular sets
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
on which the Riesz transforms
T^{m},m\in(0,n),
are bounded in
L_{2}(E)
; uniformly rectifiable sets can be built out of Lipschitz graphs and can be approximated by Lipschitz graphs at most locations and scales. David proved in
[
Dav88
]
that if
E
is
m
–uniformly rectifiable then
T^{m}
is bounded in
L^{2}(E)
. He and Semmes
[
DS91
]
conjectured that the converse is also true. That is, if
E
is an
m
–Ahlfors regular set such
T^{m}
is bounded in
L^{2}(E)
then
E
is
m
–uniformly rectifiable. The conjecture was proved by Mattila, Melnikov and Verdera in
[
MMV96
]
for
m=1
and by Nazarov, Tolsa and Volberg
[
NTV14a
]
for
m=n-1
. It remains open for integers
m\in(1,n-1)
.

Riesz transforms have also played a crucial role in characterizing removable sets for Lipschitz harmonic functions. A compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions if whenever
U\supset E
is open and
f\colon U\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz and harmonic in
U\setminus E
, then
f
is harmonic in
U
. Uy
[
Uy80
]
showed that if
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(E)=0
then
E
is removable, while
\dim_{H}(E)>n-1
implies that
E
is not removable.

Characterizing the removable sets
E
with
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(E)>0
involves the Riesz transform
T^{n-1}
. If
E
is
(n-1)
–upper regular and
T^{n-1}
is bounded on
L_{2}(E)
then
E
is
not
removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions, see
[
MP95
, Theorem 4.4]
. On the other hand, if
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(E)<\infty
and
E
is not removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions, then there exists some Borel set
F\subset E
with
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(F)>0
such that
T^{n-1}
is bounded in
L_{2}(F)
, see
[
Vol03
]
.

Due to important contributions from several people it is now known that a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(E)>0
is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions if and only if
E
is purely
(n-1)
–unrectifiable, that is,
E
intersects every
\mathcal{C}^{1}
hypersurface in a set of vanishing
(n-1)
–dimensional Hausdorff measure. One of the key ingredients in the proof of the “only if” direction is the
L_{2}(\Gamma)
–boundedness of
T^{n-1}
for Lipschitz graphs of codimension
1
. The harder “if” direction was proved by David and Mattila
[
DM00
]
(for
n=2
), and Nazarov, Tolsa and Volberg
[
NTV14a
,
NTV14b
]
for
n\geq 3
. We also mention that the
L_{2}
–boundedness of the Cauchy transform/
1
–dimensional Riesz transform was the key tool in geometrically characterizing removable sets for bounded analytic functions, see
[
Tol14
,
Ver22
]
for the long and interesting history of this problem.

There is a natural analogue of the codimension–
1
Riesz kernel in the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}
. Recall that in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
the Riesz kernel
R_{n-1}(x):=x|x|^{-n}
is a constant multiple of the gradient of the fundamental solution of the Laplacian.
Sub-Riemannian analogues of the Laplacian, known as sub-Laplacians, have been extensively studied in Carnot groups and sub-Riemannian manifolds since the early 70s and the works of Stein, Folland, and others
[
FS82
,
Fol73
,
Fol73
]
. A thorough treatment of this fully-fledged theory can be found in
[
BLU07
]
. In particular, the (canonical) sub-Laplacian in
\mathbb{H}
is defined as

\Delta_{\mathbb{H}}=X_{\mathsf{L}}^{2}+Y_{\mathsf{L}}^{2},

where

X_{\mathsf{L}}f(h):=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(h)-\frac{1}{2}y(h)\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}(h)\mbox{ and }Y_{\mathsf{L}}f(h):=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}(h)+\frac{1}{2}x(h)\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}(h)

are the left invariant vector fields which generate the horizontal distribution in
\mathbb{H}
. By a classical result of Folland
[
Fol73
]
, see also
[
BLU07
, Example 5.4.7]
, the fundamental solution of
\Delta_{\mathbb{H}}
is
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}^{-2}
where
\|\cdot\|
is the Koranyi norm in
\mathbb{H}
. One then defines the Heisenberg Riesz kernel in
\mathbb{H}
as

\mathsf{R}(x):=\frac{\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\|x\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}^{-2}}{2}=\left(\frac{x(x^{2}+y^{2})-4yz}{\|v\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}^{6}},\frac{y(x^{2}+y^{2})+4xz}{\|v\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}^{6}}\right),

where
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}f=(X_{\mathsf{L}}f,Y_{\mathsf{L}}f)
is the horizontal gradient in
\mathbb{H}
. We note that
\mathsf{R}
is a smooth,
(-3)
–homogenous, Calderón-Zygmund kernel, see Section
2.2
for more details.

Given a Radon measure in
\mathbb{H}
, the corresponding Heisenberg Riesz transform is the convolution-type singular integral formally defined by

T^{\mathsf{R}}\nu(p)=\int\mathsf{R}(y^{-1}p)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(y).

It is natural to ask whether this transform is related to rectifiability and uniform rectifiability in the same way that the Euclidean Riesz transform is, and to describe the sets
E\subset\mathbb{H}
such that
T^{\mathsf{R}}
is bounded in
L_{2}(E):=L_{2}(\mathcal{H}^{3}|_{E})
, where
E\subset\mathbb{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{3}
is the
3
–dimensional Hausdorff measure induced by the metric
d(x,y)=\|x^{-1}y\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}
.

The first difficulty in this project is defining analogues of Lipschitz graphs in
\mathbb{H}
. Unlike the Euclidean case, we cannot define Lipschitz graphs as the images of Lipschitz maps from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
\mathbb{H}
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to
\mathbb{H}
; by a result of Ambrosio and Kirchheim
[
AK00
]
,
\mathcal{H}^{3}(f(\mathbb{R}^{3}))=0
for all Lipschitz functions
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
. Franchi, Serapioni and Serra Cassano
[
FSSC06
]
introduced an
intrinsic
notion of Lipschitz graphs in Carnot groups which has been very influential in the development of sub-Riemannian geometric measure theory, see e.g.
[
SC16
,
Mat23
]
and the references therein. Intrinsic Lipschitz graphs satisfy a cone condition which will be defined in Section
2.3
. Moreover, they are
3
–Ahlfors regular and thus the question of the
L_{2}
–boundedness of the Heisenberg Riesz transform on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs makes sense.

Indeed, if
\Gamma
is an intrinsic Lipschitz graph of a bounded function and
\nu_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{H}^{3}|_{\Gamma}
the double truncations

T^{\mathsf{R}}_{r,R}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Gamma}](p):=\int_{B(p,R)\setminus B(p,r)}\mathsf{R}(y^{-1}p)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Gamma}(y)

are well defined for
f\in L_{2}(\Gamma)
,
x\in\Gamma
and
0<r<R<\infty
. As usual, we do not know
a priori
that the principal values

T^{\mathsf{R}}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Gamma}](p)=\pv(p)\int\mathsf{R}(y^{-1}p)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Gamma}(y):=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\to 0\\
R\to\infty\end{subarray}}T^{\mathsf{R}}_{r,R}[f\nu_{\Gamma}](p)

exist for
\mathcal{H}^{3}
–a.e.
p\in\Gamma
, so we say that the Heisenberg Riesz transform
T^{\mathsf{R}}
is bounded in
L_{2}(\Gamma)
if the truncations
T^{\mathsf{R}}_{r,R}
are uniformly bounded in
L_{2}(\Gamma)
; that is if there exists some
C>0
such that

\|T^{\mathsf{R}}_{r,R}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Gamma}]\|_{L_{2}(\Gamma)}\leq C\|f\|_{L_{2}(\Gamma)}

for all
f\in L_{2}(\Gamma)
and
0<r<R<\infty
.

The question of the boundedness of the Heisenberg Riesz transform was first discussed in
[
CM14
]
, where it was noted that the Heisenberg Riesz transform is
L_{2}
–bounded on the simplest examples of intrinsic Lipschitz graphs: the vertical planes (planes in
\mathbb{H}
which contain the center
\langle Z\rangle=\{(0,0,z):z\in\mathbb{R}\}
). Recently, some partial results provided hope that, as in the Euclidean case, the Heisenberg Riesz transform might be
L_{2}
–bounded on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs. First, in
[
CFO19a
]
it was shown that the Heisenberg Riesz transform is
L_{2}
–bounded on compactly supported intrinsic
C^{1,\alpha}
graphs, and in
[
FO19
]
it was shown that it is also
L_{2}
–bounded on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs of the form
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\times\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{H}
where
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
is a Euclidean Lipschitz graph in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In this paper we prove that, surprisingly and unlike the Euclidean case,
T^{\mathsf{R}}
is not
L_{2}
–bounded on certain intrinsic Lipschitz graphs.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a compactly supported intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
such that the Heisenberg Riesz transform is unbounded in
L_{2}(\Gamma)
.

We also record that if
\Gamma
is the intrinsic Lipschitz graph from Theorem
1.1
then the Heisenberg Riesz transform is unbounded in
L_{p}(\Gamma)
for all
p\in(1,\infty)
. This follows by its unboundedness in
L_{2}(\Gamma)
combined with
[
NTV98
, Theorem 1.1]
and the remark right after that theorem.

The need to characterize the lower-dimensional sets on which the (Euclidean) Riesz transform and other singular integrals are bounded in
L_{2}
led to the development of uniform rectifiability in Euclidean spaces. In the Heisenberg group, intrinsic Lipschitz graphs have been used to study rectifiability
[
MSSC10
,
FSSC11
]
and quantitative rectifiability
[
CFO19b
,
NY18
,
NY20
,
CLY22
,
FOR18
,
Rig19
]
, and, although not explicitly stated, it has been anticipated that intrinsic Lipschitz graphs should be the building blocks of uniformly rectifiable sets. Theorem
1.1
suggests that in
\mathbb{H}
, notions of uniform rectifiability based on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs and notions of uniform rectifiability based on singular integrals may diverge, and points to deep differences between the theory of uniform rectifiability in
\mathbb{H}
and its Euclidean counterpart.

On the way to proving Theorem
1.1
we also prove that the strong geometric lemma fails in the first Heisenberg group, thus further highlighting the divergence between Euclidean and Heisenberg concepts of uniform rectifiability. In order to make our statement precise we first introduce codimension–1
\beta
–numbers. If
E
is a Borel subset of the
(2n+1)
-dimensional Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}_{n}
,
x\in\mathbb{H}_{n}
, and
r>0

we define

\beta_{E}(x,r)=\inf_{L\in\mathsf{VP}}r^{-2n-1}\int_{B(x,r)\cap E}\frac{d(y,L)}{r}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2n+1}(y)

(1)

where in the infimum,
\mathsf{VP}
stands for
vertical planes
and denotes the set of codimension–
1
planes which are parallel to the
z
-axis.

In
[
CLY22
]
we proved that if
\Gamma
is an intrinsic
\lambda
–Lipschitz graph in
\mathbb{H}_{n},n\geq 2,
then, for any ball
B=B(y,R)\subset\mathbb{H}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}\int_{B\cap\Gamma}\beta_{\Gamma}(x,r)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2n+1}(x)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}r}{r}\lesssim_{\lambda}R^{2n+1}.

(2)

This is called the strong geometric lemma. (We actually established (
2
) for an
L_{2}
version of
\beta
–numbers, which easily implies (
2
) as it is stated here.)

The strong geometric lemma holds for Lipschitz graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by a result of Dorronsoro, obtained in
[
Dor85
]
, and is one of the foundations of uniform rectifiability in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In particular, an Ahlfors regular subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies a Euclidean analogue of (
2
), with constants depending only on
n
and the Ahlfors regularity constant of the set, if and only if it is uniformly rectifiable, see
[
DS91
]
.

However, the next theorem shows that the situation is very different in
\mathbb{H}_{1}
. In fact, the strong geometric lemma fails in
\mathbb{H}_{1}
for all exponents
s\in[2,4)
.

Theorem 1.2
.

There exist a constant
\lambda>0
, a radius
R>0
, and a sequence of
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz graphs
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathbf{0}\in\Gamma_{n}
for all
n
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{B(0,R)\cap\Gamma_{n}}\beta_{\Gamma_{n}}(x,r)^{s}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}(x)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}r}{r}=+\infty

for all
s\in[2,4)
.

The intrinsic Lipschitz graphs in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
are obtained by modifying a process for constructing intrinsic graphs which appeared recently in
[
NY20
, Section 3.2]
. The method introduced in
[
NY20
]
produces bumpy intrinsic graphs which are far from vertical planes at many scales. However, the intrinsic gradients of the intrinsic graphs produced in
[
NY20
]
are
L_{2}
–bounded but not bounded, so the resulting intrinsic graphs are not intrinsic Lipschitz. We overcome this obstacle by applying a stopping time argument leading to intrinsic Lipschtz graphs which retain key properties of the examples from
[
NY20
]
.

The intrinsic Lipschitz graphs that we construct are determined by the following parameters:

(1)

i\in\mathbb{N}
; the number of steps in the construction,

(2)

A\in\mathbb{N}
; the aspect ratio of the initial bumps, and

(3)

a scaling factor
\rho>1
.

In particular, our intrinsic Lipschitz graphs are intrinsic graphs of functions
f_{i,A,\rho}\colon V_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
V_{0}=\{y=0\}
and where
f_{i,A,\rho}
is supported on the unit square
[0,1]\times\{0\}\times[0,1]
.

For
i\ll A^{4}
, we show that the intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma=\Gamma_{f_{i,A,\rho}}
has many bumps at scale
r_{i}:=A^{-1}\rho^{-i}
, so

\int_{B(0,R)\cap\Gamma}\beta_{\Gamma}(x,r_{i})^{s}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}(x)\approx A^{-s}.

Since there are roughly
A^{4}
such scales, this implies Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem
1.1
takes much longer to prove and it employs several novel arguments. We first perform a “reduction to vertical planes” by proving that the principal value of singular integrals with smooth, orthogonal, and
(-3)
–homogeneous kernels on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs can be expressed as the principal value of a related singular integral on a vertical plane. This is achieved in Section
5
.

More precisely, let
\phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth and bounded intrinsic Lipschitz function with intrinsic graph
\Gamma_{\phi}
. Denote by
\Psi_{\phi}:\mathbb{H}\to\Gamma_{\phi}
the projection of
\mathbb{H}
to
\Gamma_{\phi}
along cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
. The projection restricts to a homeomorphism from
V_{0}
to
\Gamma_{\phi}
(but not a biLipschitz map), and we let
\eta_{\phi}:=(\Psi_{\phi})_{\ast}\mathcal{L}|_{V_{0}}
be the pushforward of the Lebebegue measure
\mathcal{L}|_{V_{0}}
to
\Gamma_{\phi}
. Then
\eta_{\phi}
is bounded above and below by multiples of
\mathcal{H}^{3}|_{\Gamma_{\phi}}
, see Section
2.3
. It follows from our results in Section
5
that if
g\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Borel function which is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
then

\mathsf{Rsz}_{\phi}g=T^{\mathsf{R}}[g\,\mathrm{d}\eta_{\phi}],

(3)

where
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\phi}(g)
is the
parametric Riesz transform
of
g
defined for
p\in\mathbb{H}
by

\mathsf{Rsz}_{\phi}g(p)=\pv(\Psi_{\phi}(p))\int_{\Psi_{\phi}(p)V_{0}}\mathsf{R}(\Psi_{\phi}(v)^{-1}\Psi_{\phi}(p))g(v)\,\mathrm{d}v.

(4)

We then obtain
L_{2}
bounds on the parametric Riesz transform of the identity function on the intrinsic Lipschitz graphs
f_{i,A,\rho}
produced by our construction. More precisely, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 1.3
.

There is a
\delta>0
such that for all sufficiently large
A>1
, there is a
\rho_{A}>1
such that if
N=\lfloor\delta A^{4}\rfloor
,
\phi_{A}=f_{N,A,\rho_{A}}
is the function produced in the construction of Section
3
and
U
is the unit square
[0,1]\times\{0\}\times[0,1]\subset V_{0}
, then

\|\mathsf{Rsz}_{\phi_{A}}\mathbf{1}\|_{L_{2}(U)}\gtrsim A,

where
\mathbf{1}
is the function equal to
1
on all of
\mathbb{H}
.

Proposition
1.3
is the most crucial part in the proof of Theorem
1.1
and combined with (
3
) leads relatively quickly to the proof of Theorem
1.1
; see Section
12
.

We prove Proposition
1.3
by analyzing the family of singular integrals
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha+t\gamma}
that arises from a perturbation of an intrinsic Lipschitz function
\alpha
by a smooth function
\gamma
. This requires new methods to handle the noncommutativity of
\mathbb{H}
. That is, for functions
a,b\colon\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathsf{Rsz}^{\text{Euc}}_{a}
denote the Euclidean parametric Riesz transform, defined as in (
4
). The translation-invariance of the Riesz transform implies that
\mathsf{Rsz}^{\text{Euc}}_{a}\mathbf{1}=\mathsf{Rsz}^{\text{Euc}}_{a+c}\mathbf{1}
for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
, so

\mathsf{Rsz}^{\text{Euc}}_{a+tb}\mathbf{1}=\mathsf{Rsz}^{\text{Euc}}_{a_{0}+tb_{0}}\mathbf{1},

where
a_{0}=a-a(\mathbf{0})
and
b_{0}=b-b(\mathbf{0})
both vanish at
\mathbf{0}
.

This identity does not hold in
\mathbb{H}
. In
\mathbb{H}
, translation-invariance implies that if
\Gamma_{\alpha_{1}}
is a left-translate of
\Gamma_{\alpha_{2}}
, then
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathbf{1}
is a left-translate of
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha_{2}}\mathbf{1}
. Unfortunately,
\Gamma_{\alpha+c}
is a right-translate of
\Gamma_{\alpha}
, so there is typically no relationship between
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha}\mathbf{1}
and
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha+c}\mathbf{1}
.

We solve this problem by writing
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha+t\gamma}\mathbf{1}
in two ways: first, the direct calculation (
4
), and second,
\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha+t\gamma}\mathbf{1}=\mathsf{Rsz}_{\alpha_{t}}\mathbf{1}\circ\lambda_{t}
, where each
\lambda_{t}
is a left-translation and
\alpha_{t}
is a family of functions such that
\alpha_{t}(\mathbf{0})=0
for all
t
and
\Gamma_{\alpha_{t}}=\lambda_{t}(\Gamma_{\alpha+t\gamma})
. Though these expressions represent the same function, one is easier to estimate at large scales and one is easier to estimate at small scales, and many of the bounds used in the proof of Proposition
1.3
will use one expression at large scales and the other expression at small scales.

Our results lead naturally to several new questions. For example, it is well known
[
Mat95
, Theorem 20.15]
, that if
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an
m
–dimensional Lipschitz graph and
f\in L_{1}(\Gamma)
then the principal values of the Riesz transform
T^{m}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{m}](p)
, exist for
\mathcal{H}^{m}
–a.e.
x\in\Gamma
. The proof uses that
T^{m}
is
L_{2}
–bounded
and in light of Theorem
1.1
, it is quite unclear if the same result holds in
\mathbb{H}
. We do anticipate that a modification of the construction in the current paper might be used to produce an intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
such that principal values of
T^{\mathsf{R}}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\Gamma}]
fail to exist
\nu_{\Gamma}
–a.e. for (certain) functions
f\in L_{1}
, but we will not consider this problem here.

Another interesting problem is the following. Theorem
1.1
asserts that intrinsic Lipschitz regularity is not sufficient for the
L_{2}
–boundedness of the Heisenberg Riesz transform. On the other hand, according to
[
CFO19a
]
, intrinsic
C^{1,\alpha}
regularity is indeed sufficient. Therefore, one could look for “intermediate” geometric regularity conditions on intrinsic graphs that would imply the
L^{2}
–boundedness of the Heisenberg Riesz transform. In particular, and in light of Theorem
1.2
, it would be interesting to answer the following questions:

Question 1.4
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{H}
be an intrinsic Lipschitz graph which satisfies the Carleson condition (
2
). Is it true that
T^{\mathsf{R}}
is bounded in
L_{2}(\Gamma)
?

Question 1.5
.

What natural classes of surfaces satisfy (
2
)?

The bounds in Section
6
suggest possible connections between the norm of
T^{\mathsf{R}}
and the sum of the squares of the
\beta
–numbers in (
2
); see Question
6.4
.

Finally, we note that Theorem
1.1
is related to the problem of geometrically characterizing removable sets for Lipschitz harmonic functions (RLH sets) in
\mathbb{H}
. The definition of an RLH set in
\mathbb{H}
is completely analogous to its Euclidean counterpart, except that, in
\mathbb{H}
, a function is called harmonic if it is a solution to the sub-Laplacian equation
\Delta_{\mathbb{H}}u=0
. RLH sets in Heisenberg groups were introduced in
[
CM14
]
and it was shown there that if
E\subset\mathbb{H}
is compact, then it is RLH if
\mathcal{H}^{3}(E)=0
, while it is not RLH if
\dim_{H}(E)>3
. Moreover, totally disconnected RLH sets with positive
3
-dimensional Hausdorff measure were produced in
[
CM14
,
CMT15
]
. On the other hand, it was proved in
[
CFO19a
]
that if
\mu
is a non-trivial compactly supported Radon measure in
\mathbb{H}
with
3
-upper growth, such that
T^{\mathsf{R}}
is bounded in
L_{2}(\mu)
then
\spt\mu
is not RLH. An analogous result holds in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, see
[
MP95
, Theorem 4.4]
, and combined with the
L_{2}
–boundedness of Riesz transforms on Lipschitz graphs implies that compact subsets of
1
-codimensional Lipschitz graphs with positive
(n-1)
-Hausdorff measure are not RLH. This can be used to show that if a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
H^{n-1}(E)<\infty
is RLH then it is purely
(n-1)
–unrectifiable. To our knowledge, this is the only known proof for this implication.

Theorem
1.1
shows that such a scheme cannot be used in the Heisenberg group, and naturally leads to the following fascinating question:

Question 1.6
.

Does there exist a compact subset of an intrinsic Lipschitz graph in
\mathbb{H}
with positive
3
–dimensional Hausdorff measure which is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions?

If the answer to Question
1.6
is positive it will imply that the geometric characterization of RLH sets in
\mathbb{H}
varies significantly from the analogous characterization in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. On the other hand, a negative answer to Question
1.6
would require a completely new proof method.

1.1.
Roadmap

In Section
2
, we establish some definitions and notation for the Heisenberg group and for intrinsic Lipschitz graphs. Even if the reader has seen these notions before, we introduce some new notation for intrinsic Lipschitz graphs in Section
2.3
, so we suggest that readers look through this section.

After these preliminaries, the paper can be broken into three rough parts: constructing the family of functions
f=f_{i,A,\rho}
and graphs
\Gamma=\Gamma_{i,A,\rho}
that we will use in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, proving lower bounds on the
\beta
–numbers of these surfaces, and estimating the Riesz transform on these surfaces. In Section
3
, we construct a family of intrinsic Lipschitz graphs based on the construction in
[
NY20
]
. These graphs have bumps at many different scales, and in Section
4
, we calculate the effect of these bumps on the
\beta
–numbers and prove Theorem
1.2
.

In Section
5
, we start to study the Riesz transform on
\Gamma
and other intrinsic Lipschitz graphs. Specifically, for an intrinsic Lipschitz function
\phi
, we define
\eta_{\phi}
as the pushforward of
\mathcal{L}|_{V_{0}}
as above and study the function
T\eta_{\phi}
. In general,
T\eta_{\phi}
need not be defined everywhere on
\Gamma_{\phi}
, but in Section
5
, we show that if
\phi
is smooth, bounded, and has bounded derivatives, then
T\eta_{\phi}
is defined everywhere on
\Gamma_{\phi}
.
We also introduce a singular integral operator
\tilde{T}_{\phi}
which is defined as a singular integral on a vertical plane and satisfies
\tilde{T}_{\phi}\mathbf{1}=T\eta_{\phi}
. Let
F_{\phi}:=\tilde{T}_{\phi}\mathbf{1}
.

Our main goal in these sections is to prove Proposition
1.3
. We prove Proposition
1.3
by considering the construction of
f_{i}=f_{i,A,\rho}
as a sequence of perturbations, starting with
f_{0}=0
, so that for each
i\geq 0
, we obtain
f_{i+1}
by adding bumps of scale
r_{i}:=A^{-1}\rho^{-i}
to
f_{i}
. Let
\nu_{i}=f_{i+1}-f_{i}
. Then we can prove Proposition
1.3
by bounding the derivatives
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}[F_{f_{i}+t\nu_{i}}]
and
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\,\mathrm{d}t^{2}}[F_{f_{i}+t\nu_{i}}]
and using Taylor’s theorem.

We state bounds on the derivatives of
G_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}(t):=F_{f_{i}+t\nu_{i}}
in Section
6
. Because of the scale-invariance of the Riesz transform, we can rescale
f_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
by a factor
r_{i}
to obtain functions
\alpha
and
\gamma
such that
\alpha
varies on scale roughly
\rho
and
\gamma
varies on scale roughly
1
(Section
3.1
). The derivatives of
\alpha
and
\gamma
are bounded (Lemma
3.12
and Appendix
A
), and in fact we prove bounds on derivatives of
G_{\zeta,\psi}(t)
for any functions that satisfy the same bounds.

In the remaining sections, we prove the bounds in Section
6
. First, in Section
7
, we write
G_{\zeta,\psi}^{\prime}(0)
as an integral in two ways, one which is easier to control for large scales and one for small scales (Lemma
7.2
and Lemma
7.3
). In Euclidean space, these two formulas would be the same; the difference between them comes from the noncommutativity of the Heisenberg group. We use these formulas to prove an upper bound on
G^{\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}(0)
(Lemma
7.1
).

In Section
7
, we define translation-invariant approximations of
G^{\prime}_{\zeta,\psi}
by showing that when
\lambda
is a linear function approximating
\zeta
to first order at
p
, then
G^{\prime}_{\zeta,\psi}(0)
is close to
G^{\prime}_{\lambda,\psi}(0)
on a neighborhood of
p
. We use this approximation to prove lower bounds on
G^{\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}(0)
in Section
9
and to bound inner products of the form
\langle G^{\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}(0),G^{\prime}_{f_{j},\nu_{j}}(0)\rangle
in Section
10
.

In Section
11
, we use the formulas from Section
7
again to bound
G^{\prime\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}
.
By Taylor’s theorem,

F_{f_{N}}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}G^{\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}(0)+\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}O(\|G^{\prime\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}\|_{\infty}).

Our bounds on
G^{\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}(0)
and
\langle G^{\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}(0),G^{\prime}_{f_{j},\nu_{j}}(0)\rangle
lead to a lower bound on the first term, and our bounds on
G^{\prime\prime}_{f_{i},\nu_{i}}
bound the error term. This proves Proposition
1.3
(see Section
6
for details).

Finally, in Section
12
, we use Proposition
1.3
to prove Theorem
1.1
. We first show that when
\phi_{A}
is as in Proposition
1.3
, the
L_{2}
norm of the Riesz transform on
L_{2}(\Gamma_{\phi_{A}})
is large. We then combine scaled copies of the
\Gamma_{\phi_{A}}
’s to obtain a single compactly supported intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
such that the Riesz transform is unbounded on
L_{2}(\Gamma)
, as desired.

2.
Preliminaries

Throughout this paper, we will use the notation
f\lesssim g
to denote that there is a universal constant
C>0
such that
f\leq Cg
and
f\lesssim_{a_{1},a_{2},\dots}g
to denote that there is a function
C(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)>0
such that
f\leq C(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)g
. The notation
f\approx g
is equivalent to
f\lesssim g
and
g\lesssim f
. We will also use the big–
O
notation
O(f)
to denote an error term which is at most
Cf
for some constant
C>0
and
O_{a}(f)
for an error term which is at most
C(a)f
.

2.1.
Heisenberg group

The three dimensional Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}
is the Lie group on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined by the multiplication

\displaystyle(x,y,z)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\left(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime},z+z^{\prime}+\frac{xy^{\prime}-x^{\prime}y}{2}\right)

(5)

The identity element in
\mathbb{H}
is
\mathbf{0}:=(0,0,0)
and the inverse of
v=(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{H}
is
v^{-1}:=(-x,-y,-z)
. We denote by
X=(1,0,0),Y=(0,1,0),Z=(0,0,1),
the coordinate vectors of
\mathbb{H}
and we let
x,y,z\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be the coordinate functions. The center of the group is
\langle Z\rangle=\{(0,0,z):z\in\mathbb{R}\}
. An element
v\in\mathbb{H}
is called a
horizontal vector
if
z(v)=0
, and we denote by
A
the set of horizontal vectors.

Since
\mathbb{H}
is a torsion-free nilpotent Lie group, the exponential map is a bijection between
\mathbb{H}
and the nilpotent Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}=\langle X,Y,Z\mid[X,Y]=Z\rangle
; namely,
\exp(xX+yY+zZ)=(x,y,z)
. Then (
5
) is a consequence of the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula

\exp(V)\exp(W)=\exp\left(V+W+\frac{[V,W]}{2}+\dots\right).

We will frequently identify
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathfrak{h}
and use the same notation for generators of
\mathbb{H}
and of
\mathfrak{h}
. In particular, for
V_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}
, we write the linear span of the
V_{i}
as
\langle V_{1},V_{2},\dots\rangle
, so that the set of horizontal vectors is

A=\langle X,Y\rangle=\{xX+yY\mid x,y\in\mathbb{R}\}.

Since (
5
) is based on the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula, for any
v\in\mathbb{H}
, the span
\langle v\rangle
is the one-parameter subgroup containing
v
. Since we typically write the group operation in
\mathbb{H}
as multiplication, we will often write
w^{t}=tw
for
w\in\mathbb{H}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Given an open interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, we say that
\gamma:I\to\mathbb{H}
is a
horizontal curve
if the functions
x\circ\gamma,y\circ\gamma,z\circ\gamma:I\to\mathbb{R}
are Lipschitz (hence
\gamma^{\prime}
is defined almost everywhere on
I
) and

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}s}\left[\gamma(t)^{-1}\gamma(s)\right]\big|_{s=t}\in A,

for almost every
t\in I
. Notice that left translations of horizontal curves are also horizontal.

Given
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
and
v\in\mathbb{H}
we will call the coset
L=v\langle aX+bY\rangle
a
horizontal line
. We define the
slope
of
L
as
\slope L=\frac{b}{a}
when
a\neq 0
and
\slope L=\infty
when
a=0
. This is the slope of the projection of
L
to the
xy
–plane. Note that for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
(X+\sigma Y)^{t}
is a point in the horizontal line through the origin with slope
\sigma
.

Let
X_{\mathsf{L}}
,
Y_{\mathsf{L}}
be the left-invariant vector fields

X_{\mathsf{L}}(v)=\left(1,0,-\frac{y(v)}{2}\right)\mbox{ and }Y_{\mathsf{L}}(v)=\left(0,1,\frac{x(v)}{2}\right),

and let
X_{\mathsf{R}}
,
Y_{\mathsf{R}}
be the right-invariant vector fields

X_{\mathsf{R}}(v)=\left(1,0,\frac{y(v)}{2}\right)\mbox{ and }Y_{\mathsf{R}}(v)=\left(0,1,-\frac{x(v)}{2}\right).

Note that
X_{\mathsf{L}}
and
X_{\mathsf{R}}
commute, as do
Y_{\mathsf{L}}
and
Y_{\mathsf{R}}
. We let
\partial_{x},\partial_{y},\partial_{z}:=Z
be the usual partial derivatives in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Given any vector field
\mathsf{V}=(\mathsf{V}_{x},\mathsf{V}_{y},\mathsf{V}_{z})\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
and any smooth function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we let

\mathsf{V}f(v)=\mathsf{V}\cdot\nabla f(v):=\mathsf{V}_{x}(v)\partial_{x}f(v)+\mathsf{V}_{y}(v)\partial_{y}f(v)+\mathsf{V}_{z}(v)\partial_{z}f(v).

So for example,

X_{\mathsf{L}}f(v)=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}f(vX^{t})\big|_{t=0}=\partial_{x}f(v)-\frac{y(v)}{2}Zf(v),v\in\mathbb{H}.

We also define the
horizontal gradient
of
f
as
\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}f=(X_{\mathsf{L}}f,Y_{\mathsf{L}}f)
. For clarity, we will typically use square brackets for the object of a differential operator and use
\cdot
as a low-precedence multiplication operator, so that
\mathsf{V}f\cdot\mathsf{W}g
is equal to
\mathsf{V}[f]\mathsf{W}[g]
, not
\mathsf{V}[f\mathsf{W}g]
.

The
Korányi metric
on
\mathbb{H}
is the left-invariant metric defined by

d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(v,v^{\prime}):=\|v^{-1}v^{\prime}\|_{\mathrm{Kor}},

where

\|(x,y,z)\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}:=\sqrt[4]{(x^{2}+y^{2})^{2}+16z^{2}}.

Note that
\|aX+bY\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}
, so the Korányi length of a horizontal line segment is equal to the Euclidean length of its projection to the
xy
–plane.

We also define a family of automorphisms

s_{t}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H},t\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle s_{t}(x,y,z)=(tx,ty,t^{2}z).

The mappings
s_{t}
dilate the metric; for
t\geq 0
and
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(s_{t}(p),s_{t}(p^{\prime}))=td_{\mathrm{Kor}}(p,p^{\prime}).

When
w\in A
is a horizontal vector, the one-parameter subgroup generated by
w
can be written in terms of
s_{t}
, i.e.,
s_{t}(w)=w^{t}
, but this is not true when
w
is not horizontal.

We can also define the reflection through the
z
–axis
\theta\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
by

\theta(x,y,z)=(-x,-y,z).

Note that
\theta=s_{-1}.

A
vertical plane

V
is a plane that is parallel to the
z
–axis. For any such plane, the intersection
V\cap A
is a horizontal line
v\langle aX+bY\rangle
, and we can write
V=v\langle aX+bY,Z\rangle
. We define the slope of
V
as
\slope V:=\slope(V\cap A)
.

We will frequently refer to the vertical plane
V_{0}=\{y=0\}
. We will also use the following projections. First, we define the natural (nonlinear) projection
\Pi\colon\mathbb{H}\to V_{0}
along cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
by
\Pi(v)=vY^{-y(v)},v\in\mathbb{H}
. Equivalently,

\Pi(x,y,z)=\left(x,0,z-\frac{1}{2}xy\right).

Note that
\Pi
is not a homomorphism, but it commutes with scaling because
s_{t}
sends cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
to cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, i.e.,

\Pi(s_{t}(v))=s_{t}(v)Y^{-y(s_{t}(v))}=s_{t}(v)Y^{-ty(v)}=s_{t}(vY^{-y(v)})=s_{t}(\Pi(v))

for all
v\in\mathbb{H}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Moreover, if
V
is a vertical plane which is not a coset of the
yz
–plane we define the projection
\Pi_{V}\colon\mathbb{H}\to V
along cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
by setting
\Pi_{V}(v)
to be the unique point of intersection of the coset
v\langle Y\rangle
and
V
. In particular, given
p\in\mathbb{H}
the projection
\Pi_{pV_{0}}\colon\mathbb{H}\to pV_{0}
is given by
\Pi_{pV_{0}}(v)=vY^{y(p)-y(v)},v\in\mathbb{H}
. When
\mathbf{0}\in V
, this likewise commutes with
s_{t}
.

2.2.
Kernels and symmetries

In this paper, we will consider kernels on
\mathbb{H}
which are either
\mathbb{R}
– or
\mathbb{R}^{2}
–valued continuous functions on
\mathbb{H}\backslash\{0\}
. Given a kernel
K
, let
\widehat{K}
denote the kernel
\widehat{K}(v)=K(v^{-1})
for all
v\in\mathbb{H}
. Given a Borel measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{H}
, we formally define the singular integral operator
T^{K}
by letting
T^{K}\nu(p)
be the principal value

T^{K}\nu(p):=\pv(p)\int\widehat{K}(p^{-1}w)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(w),

where

\pv(p)\int g(w)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(w):=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\to 0\\
R\to\infty\end{subarray}}\int_{B(p,R)\setminus B(p,r)}g(w)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(w).

(6)

For a Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{H}
we denote

\pv(p)\int_{A}g(w)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(w):=\pv(p)\int g(w)\mathbf{1}_{A}(w)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(w).

This definition gives rise to several operators. For
0<r<R
, we define truncated convolution operators
T^{K}_{r}
and
T^{K}_{r,R}
by

\displaystyle T_{r}^{K}\nu(p)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{H}\setminus B(p,r)}K(w^{-1}p)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(w),

\displaystyle T_{r,R}^{K}\nu(p)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B(p,R)\setminus B(p,r)}K(w^{-1}p)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(w),

for any Borel measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{H}
and any
p\in\mathbb{H}
such that these integrals are defined. Likewise we define operators
T^{K}_{\nu;r,R}f=T^{K}_{r,R}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu]
and
T^{K}_{\nu}f=T^{K}[f\,\mathrm{d}\nu]
. When
K
is understood, we will write
T=T^{K}
.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}
, a kernel is said to be
\alpha
–
homogeneous
or
of degree
\alpha
if

\displaystyle K(s_{t}(p))=t^{\alpha}K(p),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},p\in\mathbb{H}.

A function
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
\mathbb{H}
–odd
if
f(\theta(p))=-f(p)
for all
p
; it is
\mathbb{H}
–even
if
f(\theta(p))=f(p)
for all
p
, and since
\theta=s_{-1}
, a homogeneous kernel is
\mathbb{H}
–odd or
\mathbb{H}
–even if it is homogeneous for an odd or even power, respectively.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
W
be a left-invariant vector field corresponding to a horizontal element of
\mathbb{H}
.
If
K
is
\alpha
–homogeneous, then
WK
is
(\alpha-1)
–homogeneous.

Proof.

Suppose that
K
is
\alpha
–homogeneous. Let
u\in\mathbb{H}
be an element of norm 1 and let
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\displaystyle WK(u)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{K(uW^{h})-K(u)}{h}

\displaystyle=t^{-\alpha}\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{K(s_{t}(u)W^{th})-K(s_{t}(u))}{h}

\displaystyle=t^{-\alpha+1}\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{K(s_{t}(u)W^{th})-K(s_{t}(u))}{th}

\displaystyle=t^{-\alpha+1}WK(s_{t}(u)).

We now get that
WK
is
(\alpha-1)
–homogeneous.
∎

Likewise, derivatives of
\mathbb{H}
–odd kernels are
\mathbb{H}
–even and vice versa.

Given an orthogonal matrix
M\in\mathrm{O}(2)
,
M
acts on
\mathbb{H}
as an isometry

\tilde{M}(x,y,z)=(M(x,y),\det(M)z).

Given an
\mathbb{R}^{2}
–valued kernel, we say that it is
orthogonal
if

\displaystyle K(\tilde{M}(p))=M(K(p))

for all
p\in\mathbb{H}
and all
M\in\mathrm{O}(2)
.

We now define a specific kernel that is the main object of our study. Let
\Psi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\Psi(v)=\|v\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}^{-2}
. By a celebrated result of Folland, see
[
Fol73
]
and
[
CDPT07
, Theorem 5.15]
, we know that the fundamental solution of the sub-Laplacian equation

X_{\mathsf{L}}^{2}+Y_{\mathsf{L}}^{2}=0,

is
(8\pi)^{-1}\Psi
. Analogously to the Euclidean case, the Riesz kernel
\mathsf{R}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}(v)

\displaystyle:=-\nabla_{\mathbb{H}}\Psi=-\left(X_{\mathsf{L}}\Psi,Y_{\mathsf{L}}\Psi\right)=\left(\frac{2x(x^{2}+y^{2})-8yz}{\|v\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}^{6}},\frac{2y(x^{2}+y^{2})+8xz}{\|v\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}^{6}}\right).

(7)

Since
\Psi
is symmetric around the origin and homogeneous of degree
-2
, its gradient
\mathsf{R}(v)
is an
\mathbb{H}
–odd orthogonal kernel of degree
-3
. The smoothness and the
-3
–homogeneity of
\mathsf{R}
easily imply that it is a
3
–dimensional standard Calderón–Zygmund kernel, see e.g.
[
Chr90
, Chapter 6]
. Therefore, if
\nu
is a Borel measure on
\mathbb{H}
such that

\nu(B(x,r))\leq Cr^{3},\forall x\in\mathbb{H},r>0,

then

|T_{r}^{\mathsf{R}}(f\,\mathrm{d}\nu)|<\infty
for
f\in L_{p}(\nu),p\in[1,\infty)
and
|T_{r,R}^{\mathsf{R}}(f\,\mathrm{d}\nu)|<\infty
for
f\in L_{p}(\nu),p\in[1,\infty]
. In fact, truncated singular integrals (with respect to
\nu
) are finite for any Borel kernel which satisfies
|K(v)|\lesssim\|v\|^{-3},v\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\{0\}
.

2.3.
Intrinsic graphs and intrinsic Lipschitz graphs

In previous papers, intrinsic graphs have been defined as graphs of functions from the vertical plane
V_{0}=\langle X,Z\rangle
to
\mathbb{R}
. In this paper, we introduce new notation that defines them in terms of functions from
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathbb{R}
that are constant along cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
. Any function from
V_{0}
to
\mathbb{R}
can be extended to a function that is constant along cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, so the two definitions give the same class of graphs, but this definition streamlines some notation. Di Donato and Le Donne have used similar techniques to define intrinsically Lipschitz sections in
[
DDLD22
]
.

For any function
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
which is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, we define the
intrinsic graph
of
f
as

\Gamma_{f}=\{vY^{f(v)}\mid v\in V_{0}\}=\{p\in\mathbb{H}\mid f(p)=y(p)\}.

We define
\Psi_{f}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\Gamma_{f}
by
\Psi_{f}(p)=pY^{f(p)-y(p)}
for all
p
.
This map projects
\mathbb{H}
to
\Gamma_{f}
along cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
. It is constant along cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
and satisfies

y(\Psi_{f}(p))=f(p)\text{\qquad for all $p\in\mathbb{H}$}.

(8)

Left-translations and scalings of intrinsic graphs are also intrinsic graphs, and we can use (
8
) to determine the corresponding functions.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function which is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
and let
g\in\mathbb{H}
. Let
h\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
,

h(p)=y(g)+f(g^{-1}p).

Then
h
is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
and satisfies
\Gamma_{h}=g\Gamma_{f}
and
\Psi_{h}(p)=g\Psi_{f}(g^{-1}p)

for any
p\in\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

Since
\Psi_{f}
is the unique map from
\mathbb{H}
to
\Gamma_{f}
that satisfies
\Psi_{f}(p)\langle Y\rangle=p\langle Y\rangle
for all
p\in\mathbb{H}
, the map
\hat{\Psi}(p)=g\Psi_{f}(g^{-1}p)
sends
\mathbb{H}
to
g\Gamma_{f}
and satisfies

\hat{\Psi}(p)\langle Y\rangle=g\Psi_{f}(g^{-1}p)\langle Y\rangle=gg^{-1}p\langle Y\rangle=p\langle Y\rangle

for all
p\in\mathbb{H}
. Therefore,
\hat{\Psi}=\Psi_{h}
where

h(p)=y(\hat{\Psi}(p))=y(g\Psi_{f}(g^{-1}p))=y(g)+f(g^{-1}p)

and
g\Gamma_{f}=\Gamma_{h}
.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function which is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
. Let
t\neq 0
and let
h\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
,

h(p)=tf(s_{t}^{-1}(p)).

Then
\Gamma_{h}=s_{t}(\Gamma_{f})
and
\Psi_{h}(p)=s_{t}(\Psi_{f}(s_{t}^{-1}(p)))

for any
p\in\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

As above,
\hat{\Psi}(p)=s_{t}(\Psi_{f}(s_{t}^{-1}(p)))
has image
s_{t}(\Gamma_{f})
and satisfies

\hat{\Psi}(p)\langle Y\rangle=s_{t}(\Psi_{f}(s_{t}^{-1}(p)))\langle Y\rangle=s_{t}(s_{t}^{-1}(p)\langle Y\rangle)=p\langle Y\rangle

for all
p\in\mathbb{H}
. Therefore,
\hat{\Psi}=\Psi_{h}
where

h(p)=y(\hat{\Psi}(p))=tf(s_{t}^{-1}(p))

and
s_{t}(\Gamma_{f})=\Gamma_{h}
.
∎

Let
X_{\mathsf{L}}=X
and
X_{\mathsf{R}}=X_{\mathsf{L}}-yZ
be the left-invariant and right-invariant vector fields defined in Section
2.1
. For a smooth function
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have
X_{\mathsf{L}}[f](v)=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}f(vX^{t})|_{t=0}
and
X_{\mathsf{R}}[f](v)=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}f(X^{t}v)|_{t=0}
.
If
\phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, we define the intrinsic gradient
\nabla_{\phi}
as the vector field

\nabla_{\phi}(p)=X_{\mathsf{R}}(p)-\phi(p)Z(p)=X_{\mathsf{L}}(p)+(y(p)-\phi(p))Z.

(9)

When
v\in V_{0}
, this agrees with the usual definition of the intrinsic gradient

\nabla_{\phi}(v)=X_{\mathsf{L}}(v)-\phi(v)Z(v)
; equation (
9
) is the extension of
\nabla_{\phi}
that is right-invariant with respect to the action of
\langle Y\rangle
.
If
\phi
and
\beta
are smooth and constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, then for all
p\in\mathbb{H}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\nabla_{\phi}\beta(pY^{t})=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}u}\beta((X-\phi(pY^{t})Z)^{u}pY^{t})\big|_{u=0}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}u}\beta((X-\phi(p)Z)^{u}p)\big|_{u=0}=\nabla_{\phi}\beta(p),

so
\nabla_{\phi}\beta
is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
.

The intrinsic gradient
\nabla_{\phi}
can also be interpreted in terms of the horizontal curves that foliate
\Gamma_{\phi}
. When
\phi
is smooth, the restriction of
\nabla_{\phi}
to
V_{0}
is the smooth vector field
\nabla_{\phi}(v)=X_{\mathsf{L}}-\phi(v)Z
. It follows that
V_{0}
is foliated by integral curves of
\nabla_{\phi}
; we call these the
characteristic curves
of
\Gamma_{\phi}
. If
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to V_{0}
is such a curve then
\gamma=\Psi_{\phi}\circ g
is a horizontal curve in
\Gamma_{\phi}
with

\gamma^{\prime}(t)=X_{\mathsf{L}}+\nabla_{\phi}\phi(\gamma(t))Y_{\mathsf{L}},

(10)

and the following lemma holds.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\phi,m\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be smooth functions which are constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, let
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to V_{0}
be a characteristic curve of
\Gamma_{\phi}
, and let
\gamma=\Psi_{\phi}\circ g
. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and any
k\geq 1
,

\nabla^{k}_{\phi}m(\gamma(t))=\nabla^{k}_{\phi}m(g(t))=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\,\mathrm{d}t^{k}}[m\circ\gamma(t)]=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\,\mathrm{d}t^{k}}[m\circ g(t)].

Proof.

Since
g
is an integral curve of
\nabla_{\phi}
, we have

\nabla^{k}_{\phi}m(g(t))=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\,\mathrm{d}t^{k}}[m\circ g(t)]

for any
k\geq 1
. Since
m
and
\nabla^{k}m
are constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, we have
\nabla^{k}_{\phi}m(\gamma(t))=\nabla^{k}_{\phi}m(g(t))
and
m\circ g=m\circ\gamma
, which implies the lemma.
∎

In particular, if
\gamma
is as above, then
\nabla_{\phi}\phi(\gamma(0))
is the slope of the tangent line to
\gamma
at
\gamma(0)
. This implies that the intrinsic gradient is invariant under translations and scalings. That is, if
\Gamma_{\hat{\phi}}=gs_{t}(\Gamma_{\phi})
, then
\nabla_{\hat{\phi}}\hat{\phi}(gs_{t}(p))=\nabla_{\phi}\phi(p)
.

For
0<\lambda<1
, we define the open double cone

\Cone_{\lambda}=\{p\in\mathbb{H}\mid\lambda d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(\mathbf{0},p)<|y(p)|\}.

This is a scale-invariant cone, and when
\lambda
is close to
1
, it is a small neighborhood of
\langle Y\rangle\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
. An intrinsic graph
\Gamma_{\phi}
is a
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz graph
if
p\Cone_{\lambda}\cap\Gamma_{\phi}=\emptyset
for all
p\in\Gamma_{\phi}
. Equivalently,
\Gamma_{\phi}
is
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz if and only if
\Lip(y|_{\Gamma_{\phi}})\leq\lambda
. If
\phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
and
\Gamma_{\phi}
is a
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz graph, we say that
\phi
is a
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz function
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\lambda\in(0,1)
and let
\Gamma_{f}
be a
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz graph. Then

\displaystyle|y(v)-f(v)|\approx_{\lambda}d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(v,\Gamma_{f}),\qquad\forall v\in\mathbb{H}.

Proof.

On one hand,
d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(v,\Gamma_{f})\leq d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(v,\Psi_{f}(v))=|y(v)-f(v)|
, so it suffices to show that
d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(v,\Gamma_{f})\gtrsim|y(v)-f(v)|
. It suffices to show that there is some
C>0
depending only on
\lambda
such that
d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(pY^{\alpha},\Gamma_{f})\geq C|\alpha|
for all
p\in\Gamma_{f}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
; the lemma then follows by taking
p=\Psi_{f}(v)
and
\alpha=y(v)-f(v)
.

Let
C=\frac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}
, so that
\lambda=\frac{1-C}{1+C}
. Let
B(Y,C)
be the open ball of radius
C
around
Y
. If
q\in B(Y,C)
, then

\lambda d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(\mathbf{0},q)<\lambda(1+C)=1-C<|y(q)|,

so
q\in\Cone_{\lambda}
. Since
\Cone_{\lambda}
is scale-invariant, this implies that
B(Y^{\alpha},C|\alpha|)\subset\Cone_{\lambda}
.

By the intrinsic Lipschitz condition,
\Gamma_{f}\cap p\Cone_{\lambda}=\emptyset
, so

\Gamma_{f}\cap pB(Y^{\alpha},C|\alpha|)=\Gamma_{f}\cap B(pY^{\alpha},C|\alpha|)=\emptyset.

Therefore,
d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(pY^{\alpha},\Gamma_{f})\geq C|\alpha|
, as desired.
∎

By
[
CMPSC14
]
, if
\Gamma_{\phi}
is
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz, then
\|\nabla_{\phi}\phi\|_{\infty}
is bounded by a function of
\lambda
. Indeed,

\|\nabla_{\phi}\phi\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{1-\lambda^{2}}},

(11)

see
[
NY20
, Sec. 2.2]
.
Conversely, if
\phi
is defined on all of
\mathbb{H}
and
\nabla_{\phi}\phi
is bounded, then
\phi
is
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz for some
0<\lambda<1
depending on
\|\nabla_{\phi}\phi\|_{\infty}

[
CMPSC14
]
.

When
\phi
is smooth and
p\in\Gamma_{\phi}
, we define the
tangent plane
to
\Gamma_{\phi}
at
p
to be the vertical plane
P_{p}=p\langle X+\nabla_{\phi}\phi(p)Y,Z\rangle
with slope
\nabla_{\phi}\phi(p)
. For
t>0
,
ps_{t}(p^{-1}\Gamma_{\phi})
is the scaling of
\Gamma_{\phi}
centered at
p
, and as
t\to\infty
,
ps_{t}(p^{-1}\Gamma_{\phi})
converges to
P_{p}
. More generally, when
\phi
is intrinsic Lipschitz, a Rademacher-type theorem holds for almost every
p\in\Gamma_{\phi}
, so the definition of
\nabla_{\phi}\phi(p)
can be extended so that
ps_{t}(p^{-1}\Gamma_{\phi})
converges to
P_{p}
for almost every
p\in\Gamma_{\phi}

[
FSSC01
]
.

The following lemma, based on Lemma 2.3 of
[
NY20
]
, is helpful for bounding intrinsic Lipschitz functions.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
and let
\psi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz function. Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\psi}
. Let
g\in\Gamma
. For any
h\in\mathbb{H}
,

|\psi(g)-\psi(h)|\leq\frac{2}{1-\lambda}d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(g,h\langle Y\rangle)\leq\frac{2}{1-\lambda}d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(g,h).

(12)

Furthermore, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and any
p\in\mathbb{H}
,

|\psi(p)-\psi(pZ^{t})|\leq\frac{4\sqrt{|t|}}{1-\lambda}.

Proof.

Since
\psi
is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
, it suffices to prove (
12
) when
h\in\Gamma
.
Let
m=d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(g,h\langle Y\rangle)
. Let
c\in h\langle Y\rangle
be such that
d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(g,c)=m
. By the intrinsic Lipschitz condition,

|y(h)-y(c)|\leq|y(h)-y(g)|+m\leq\lambda d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(g,h)+m\leq\lambda(m+|y(h)-y(c)|)+m.

This simplifies to give

|y(h)-y(c)|\leq\frac{1+\lambda}{1-\lambda}m,

and thus

|\psi(g)-\psi(h)|=|y(g)-y(h)|\leq|y(g)-y(c)|+|y(c)-y(h)|\leq\frac{2m}{1-\lambda}.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and any
p\in\mathbb{H}
,

|\psi(p)-\psi(pZ^{t})|=|\psi(\Psi_{\psi}(p))-\psi(\Psi_{\psi}(p)Z^{t})|\leq\frac{2}{1-\lambda}\|Z^{t}\|_{\mathrm{Kor}}=\frac{4\sqrt{|t|}}{1-\lambda}.

∎

This implies the following lemma, whose proof we omit; see also
[
FS16
]
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\phi
be a
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz function, let
p\in\Gamma_{\phi}
, and let
r>0
. There is a
c>0
depending on
\lambda
such that

\Pi(B(p,cr))\subset\Pi(B(p,r)\cap\Gamma_{\phi})\subset\Pi(B(p,r)).

In particular,
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\Pi(B(p,r)))\approx\mathcal{L}(\Pi(B(p,r)))\approx r^{3}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is Lebesgue measure on
V_{0}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

There is a left-invariant Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{H}
such that
\mu(S)=\mathcal{L}(\Pi(S))
for any intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
and any Borel set
S\subset\Gamma
.
Further, if
m\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Borel function which is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
and
S\subset\Gamma
is Borel, then

\int_{S}m(v)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(v)=\int_{\Pi(S)}m(v)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(v)

(13)

if the integrals exist. If
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
\Gamma
is
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz, then
\mathcal{H}^{3}(S)\approx_{\lambda}\mu(S)
. In particular,
\mu|_{\Gamma}
is Ahlfors
3
–regular with constants only depending on
\lambda
.

This will be our “default” measure on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs, and we will abbreviate
\,\mathrm{d}\mu(v)
by
\,\mathrm{d}v
.

Proof.

For
S\subset\mathbb{H}
, let

\mu(S)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\inf_{C_{\epsilon}(S)}\sum_{U\in C_{\epsilon}(S)}\mathcal{L}(\Pi(U))

(14)

where
C_{\epsilon}(S)
is the set of covers of
S
by sets of diameter at most
\epsilon
. This is a Borel measure on
\mathbb{H}
by
[
Mat95
, Theorem 4.2]
, and the restriction of
\mu
to any intrinsic Lipschitz graph is the pullback of
\mathcal{L}|_{V_{0}}
, i.e.,
\mu(S)=\mathcal{L}(\Pi(S))
for any intrinsic Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
and any Borel set
S\subset\Gamma
. Consequently,
\mu
satisfies (
13
).

By the area formula,
[
CMPSC14
, Theorem 1.6]
, if
\phi
is a
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz function and
S\subset\Gamma_{\phi}
is Borel, then

\mathcal{H}^{3}(S)\approx\int_{\Pi(S)}\sqrt{1+(\nabla_{\phi}\phi(v))^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}v=\int_{S}\sqrt{1+(\nabla_{\phi}\phi(v))^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}v\approx_{\lambda}\mu(S).

Since, by
[
FS16
, Theorem 3.9]
,
\mathcal{H}^{3}|_{\Gamma_{\phi}}
is an Ahlfors 3–regular measure this implies that
\mu|_{\Gamma_{\phi}}
is also Ahlfors
3
–regular with constants only depending on
\lambda
.

Finally, we check that
\mu
is left-invariant. It suffices to show that
\mathcal{L}(\Pi(gU))=\mathcal{L}(\Pi(U))
for any
g\in\mathbb{H}
and any Borel set
U\subset\mathbb{H}
. First, for any
g,h\in\mathbb{H}
,

\Pi(gh)=ghY^{-y(g)-y(h)}=g\left(h\cdot Y^{-y(h)}\right)Y^{-y(g)}=\Pi(g\Pi(h)).

Let
\beta_{g}\colon V_{0}\to V_{0}
,
\beta_{g}(v)=\Pi(gv)
, so that
\Pi(gU)=\beta_{g}(\Pi(U))
. Let
g=(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{H}
and
v=(x^{\prime},0,z^{\prime})\in V_{0}
. Then

\beta_{g}(v)=\Pi(gv)=gvY^{-y}=\left(x+x^{\prime},0,z+z^{\prime}-yx^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}xy\right).

That is,
\beta_{g}
is an affine transformation of
V_{0}
with determinant
1
. Thus
\mathcal{L}(\Pi(gU))=\mathcal{L}(\beta_{g}(\Pi(U)))=\mathcal{L}(\Pi(U))
. By (
14
),
\mu
is a left-invariant measure.
∎

2.4.
Taylor series estimates

In this section, we prove a Taylor-type estimate for functions on intrinsic Lipschitz graphs, which we will use extensively in the rest of the paper. Let
a
be a smooth intrinsic Lipschitz function and let
m
be a smooth function which is constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
. (In particular, we can take
m=a
.) We will show that
m
is close to a constant function or an affine function when the derivatives
\nabla_{a}m
,
\nabla_{a}^{2}m
, and
Zm
are small.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
0<\lambda<1
and let
a\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth
\lambda
–intrinsic Lipschitz function.
Let
m\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function. Suppose that
a
and
m
are constant on cosets of
\langle Y\rangle
. Let
p\in\Gamma_{a}
and let
q\in\mathbb{H}
. Let
r=d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(p,q)
,
L=\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{1-\lambda^{2}}}
, and
B=B(p,2(L+1)r))
. Then

m(q)=m(p)+O_{\lambda}\left(r\|\nabla_{a}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}+r^{2}\|\partial_{z}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}\right)

and

m(q)=m(p)+(x(q)-x(p))\nabla_{a}m(p)+O_{\lambda}\left(r^{2}\big[\|\nabla_{a}^{2}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}+\|\partial_{z}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}\big]\right).

In particular, if
\mathbf{0}\in\Gamma_{a}
and
p=\mathbf{0}
, then
a(\mathbf{0})=0
, so

\displaystyle|a(q)+a(\theta(q))|

\displaystyle=O_{\lambda}\left(r^{2}(\|\nabla_{a}^{2}a\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}+\|\partial_{z}a\|_{L_{\infty}(B)})\right),

where
\theta(x,y,z)=(-x,-y,z)
.

Proof.

By (
11
), we have
\|\nabla_{a}a\|_{\infty}\leq L
.
Let
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\Gamma_{a}
be a horizontal curve through
p
. We parametrize
\gamma
so that
x(\gamma(t))=t
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular
\gamma(x(p))=p
. By (
10
),
\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|\leq L+1
for all
t
, so
\Lip(\gamma)\leq L+1
. In particular,
\gamma(x(q))\in B(p,(L+1)r)
.

Recall that
\Pi_{pV_{0}}\colon\mathbb{H}\to pV_{0}
is the projection
\Pi_{pV_{0}}(s)=sY^{y(p)-y(s)}
; for any
s\in\mathbb{H}
, we have

d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(p,\Pi_{pV_{0}}(s))\leq d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(p,s)+|y(p)-y(s)|\leq 2d_{\mathrm{Kor}}(p,s).

Then
g^{\prime}:=\Pi_{pV_{0}}(\gamma(x(q)))
and
q^{\prime}:=\Pi_{pV_{0}}(q)
are two points in
B\cap pV_{0}
with the same
x
–coordinate, so
g^{\prime}=q^{\prime}Z^{z_{0}}
for some
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|z_{0}|\lesssim_{\lambda}r^{2}
. Since
m(q)=m(q^{\prime})
and
m(\gamma(x(q)))=m(g^{\prime})
,

m(q)=m(\gamma(x(q)))+O_{\lambda}(r^{2}\|\partial_{z}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}).

Since
(m\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(t)=\nabla_{a}m(\gamma(t))
and
(m\circ\gamma)^{\prime\prime}(t)=\nabla^{2}_{a}m(\gamma(t))
,
the Mean Value Theorem implies that

m(\gamma(x(q)))=m(p)+O(r\|\nabla_{a}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}),

so

m(q)=m(p)+O_{\lambda}\left(r\|\nabla_{a}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}+r^{2}\|\partial_{z}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}\right).

Taylor’s theorem implies

m(\gamma(x(q)))=m(p)+(x(q)-x(p))\nabla_{a}m(p)+O_{\lambda}(r^{2}\|\nabla_{a}^{2}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}),

so

m(q)=m(p)+(x(q)-x(p))\nabla_{a}m(p)+O_{\lambda}\left(r^{2}(\|\nabla_{a}^{2}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)}+\|\partial_{z}m\|_{L_{\infty}(B)})\right),

as desired.
∎

3.
Construction

In this section, we construct the family of graphs that we will study in the rest of this paper. Our construction is based on the construction in Section 3.2 of
[
NY20
]
. The authors of
[
NY20
]
introduced a process to construct an intrinsic graph
\Gamma_{\psi}
that is far from a vertical plane at many scales (see Proposition 3.4 of
[
NY20
]
). Unfortunately for our purposes, the intrinsic gradient
\nabla_{\psi}\psi
is
L_{2}
–bounded but not bounded, so
\Gamma_{\phi}
is not intrinsic Lipschitz. In this section, we will modify that construction via a stopping time argument so that it produces an intrinsic Lipschitz function with similar properties. To keep this paper self-contained, we will reproduce the construction of
[
NY20
]
in parallel with our modification.

The construction depends on three parameters: an integer aspect ratio
A>1
, an integer scale factor
\rho>1
, and a number of steps
i
. In
[
NY20
]
, one starts with a function
\psi_{0}=0
and constructs
\psi_{i+1}
by perturbing
\psi_{i}
. The difference
\psi_{i+1}-\psi_{i}
is a sum of bump functions supported on regions in
V_{0}
with aspect ratio
A
, and the scale of the perturbations decreases by a factor of
\rho
at each step.

Recall that if
\psi\colon V_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function, then it induces a smooth vector field
\nabla_{\psi}=\partial_{x}-\psi\partial_{z}
on
V_{0}
, and we call integral curves of
\nabla_{\psi}
characteristic curves. Since
\psi
is smooth, there is a unique characteristic curve of
\Gamma_{\psi}
through each point of
V_{0}
.
A
pseudoquad

Q\subset V_{0}
for
\Gamma_{\psi}
is a region of the form

Q=\left\{(x,0,z)\in V_{0}\mid x\in[a,b],z\in[g_{1}(x),g_{2}(x)]\right\}

where
g_{1},g_{2}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
are functions whose graphs are characteristic curves, i.e.,
g_{i}^{\prime}(x)=-\psi(x,0,g_{i}(x))
for all
x
. In particular,
g_{i}\in C^{1}([a,b])
. We define the
width
of
Q
to be
\delta_{x}(Q)=b-a
and we define the
height
to be
\delta_{z}(Q)=g_{2}(a)-g_{1}(a)
. Since the distance between the top and bottom boundary varies, there is no single canonical height, but this choice is enough for many applications. The
aspect ratio
of
Q
is the ratio
\frac{\delta_{x}(Q)}{\sqrt{\delta_{z}(Q)}}
; the square root in the denominator makes this ratio scale-invariant.

We say that two pseudoquads are
disjoint
if and only if their interiors are disjoint. We say that
U=Q_{1}\cup\dots\cup Q_{n}
is a
partition
of
U
if the
Q_{i}
’s are disjoint.

Let
U=[0,1]\times\{0\}\times[0,1]
and let
\kappa\colon[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a nonnegative smooth function with
\supp\kappa\subset(0,1)^{2}
. We require that
\|\kappa\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,
\kappa(s,t)>0
for
s,t\in[\frac{1}{5},\frac{4}{5}]
, and that the partial derivatives of
\kappa
of order at most 2 are all in the interval
[-1,1]
. (The assumption on partial derivatives is used in
[
NY20
]
to bound certain derivatives when
\rho\geq 8
; it can be dropped at the cost of changing some constants.)

We will use induction to construct functions
f_{i}
and
\psi_{i}
supported on
U
. We start with
f_{0}=0
and
\psi_{0}=0
, and for each
i\geq 0
, we let
r_{i}:=A^{-1}\rho^{-i}
and construct:

•

a partition
U=Q_{i,1}\cup\dots\cup Q_{i,k_{i}}
such that each
Q_{i,j}
is a pseudoquad for
\Gamma_{\psi_{i}}
with width
\delta_{x}(Q_{i,j})=Ar_{i}
, height
\delta_{z}(Q_{i,j})=r_{i}^{2}
, and aspect ratio
A
,

•

a collection of bump functions
\kappa_{i,j}
such that
\kappa_{i,j}
is supported on
Q_{i,j}
and
\|\kappa_{i,j}\|_{\infty}\approx A^{-1}r_{i}
,

•

a set
J_{i}\subset\{1,\dots,k_{i}\}
such that
|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
on
Q_{i,j}
for every
j\in J_{i}
. Furthermore, we let
S_{i}:=\bigcup_{j\in\{1,\dots,k_{i}\}\setminus J_{i}}Q_{i,j}
and require that
S_{i}\supset S_{i-1}
(where
S_{-1}=\emptyset
).

We then define
\kappa_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\kappa_{i,j}
,

\nu_{i}:=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}\kappa_{i,j}=\mathbf{1}_{S_{i}^{c}}\kappa_{i},

\psi_{i+1}:=\psi_{i}+\kappa_{i}
and
f_{i+1}:=f_{i}+\nu_{i}
. The
\psi_{i}
’s are the functions constructed in
[
NY20
]
, and the
f_{i}
’s are a “stopped” version of the
\psi_{i}
’s. That is, when
|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}|
gets too large on a pseudoquad, that pseudoquad is added to
S_{i}
, and the construction ensures that
f_{k}|_{S_{i}}=f_{i}|_{S_{i}}
for all
k>i
.

We first construct the
Q_{i,j}
’s. Suppose that we have already defined
f_{i}
. Let

\displaystyle G_{i}:=\left\{\left(mAr_{i},0,nr_{i}^{2}\right):m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\},

let
k_{i}=A^{-1}r_{i}^{3}
, and let
v_{i,1},v_{i,2},\dots,v_{i,k_{i}}
be an enumeration of
G_{i}\cap\big([0,1)\times\{0\}\times[0,1)\big)
. Let
\Phi(\psi_{i})_{s}
be the flow map of
\nabla_{\psi_{i}}
on
V_{0}
; so that
\Phi(\psi_{i})_{0}(v)=v
for all
v\in V_{0}
and the map
s\mapsto\Phi(\psi_{i})_{s}(v)
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
is a characteristic curve of
\Gamma_{\psi_{i}}
. In particular,
x(\Phi(\psi_{i})_{s}(v))=x(v)+s
. Let

\displaystyle R_{i,j}(s,t):=\Phi(\psi_{i})_{s}(v_{i,j}Z^{t})

and let

\displaystyle Q_{i,j}:=R_{i,j}([0,Ar_{i}]\times[0,r_{i}^{2}]).

This is a pseudoquad of width
Ar_{i}
and height
r_{i}^{2}
. Because the top and bottom edges of
U
are characteristic curves of
\psi_{i}
, we have
Q_{i,j}\subset U
for all
j
. Indeed,
U=Q_{i,1}\cup\dots\cup Q_{i,k_{i}}
is a locally finite partition of
U
. (Local finiteness follows for instance from Lemma
3.8
.) Let
\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\{Q_{i,1},\dots,Q_{i,k_{i}}\}
.

For each
Q_{i,j}
, we define

\displaystyle\kappa_{i,j}(R_{i,j}(s,t)):=A^{-1}r_{i}\kappa(A^{-1}r_{i}^{-1}s,r_{i}^{-2}t),

(15)

and let
\kappa_{i}:=\sum_{j}\kappa_{i,j}
. Note that
\kappa_{i}
is smooth and that it is zero in a neighborhood of
\partial Q_{i,j}
for each
j
.

To define
S_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
, we will need some notation.
For every
k
, we say that a pseudoquad
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k+1}
is a
child
of
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}
if
\interior(Q^{\prime})\cap\interior(Q)\neq\emptyset
.
Note that this does not necessarily mean that
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
; the pseudoquads in
\mathcal{Q}_{k+1}
do not subdivide the pseudoquads in
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
.
Nonetheless, by the local finiteness of
\mathcal{Q}_{k+1}
, every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}
has only finitely many children.

Let
\mathcal{C}(Q)
be the set of children of
Q
and define
\mathcal{C}^{n}(Q)
inductively so that
\mathcal{C}^{0}(Q)=\{Q\}
and
\mathcal{C}^{n}(Q)=\bigcup_{Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{n-1}(Q)}\mathcal{C}(Q^{\prime})
. For any set
\mathcal{M}
of pseudoquads, we let

\bigcup(\mathcal{M}):=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{M}}Q.

For
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}
and
l>k
, let

Q^{(l)}=\bigcup(\mathcal{C}^{l-k}(Q)).

Let
\mathcal{D}(Q)=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{C}^{n}(Q)
be the set of
descendants
of
Q
.

If
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}
and
v\in\interior(Q)
, then any neighborhood of
v
intersects the interior of some child of
Q
. It follows that
v
lies in the closure of
\bigcup(\mathcal{C}(Q))
, and since
\mathcal{C}(Q)
is finite,
v\in\bigcup(\mathcal{C}(Q))
. Since the closure of
\interior(Q)
is
Q
, we have
Q\subset\bigcup(\mathcal{C}(Q))
; in fact,
\bigcup(\mathcal{C}^{n}(Q))\subset\bigcup(\mathcal{C}^{n+1}(Q))
for all
n
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{i}
be the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{i}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{S}_{i-1}}\mathcal{C}(Q)\cup\left\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}:\max_{x\in Q_{i,j}}|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(x)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\right\},

where
\mathcal{S}_{-1}=\emptyset
. Let
S_{i}=\bigcup(\mathcal{S}_{i})
and let
J_{i}=\{j:Q_{i,j}\not\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\}
.
Then
S_{i+1}\supset S_{i}
for all
i
.
Let
\nu_{i}:=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}\kappa_{i,j}=\mathbf{1}_{S_{i}^{c}}\kappa_{i}

Since
\kappa_{i,j}
is zero on a neighborhood of
\partial Q_{i,j}
, this is smooth. We define
f_{i+1}=f_{i}+\nu_{i}
and
\psi_{i+1}=\psi_{i}+\kappa_{i}
.

Note that
S_{i}\subset S_{j}
for all
i<j
, so
\nu_{i}
is zero on a neighborhood of
S_{i}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle f_{j}|_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle=f_{i}|_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle\nabla_{f_{j}}f_{j}|_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}|_{S_{i}}.

(16)

Conversely, if
v\not\in S_{i-1}
, then for all
k\leq i-1
,
v\not\in S_{k}
and
\kappa_{k}(v)=\nu_{k}(v)
, so

f_{i}|_{S_{i-1}^{c}}=\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}\nu_{k}|_{S_{i-1}^{c}}=\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}\kappa_{k}|_{S_{i-1}^{c}}=\psi_{i}|_{S_{i-1}^{c}}.

(17)

The functions
\psi_{i}
are exactly the same as those defined in Section 3.2 of
[
NY20
]
and our
\kappa_{i}
correspond to their
\nu_{i}
. We will show that if
\rho
is sufficiently large,
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small, and
i<\epsilon N^{4}
, then
f_{i}
is intrinsic Lipschitz and the set on which
f_{i}
and
\psi_{i}
differ is small.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
A>1
be sufficiently large. If
\rho
is sufficiently large (depending on
A
), then for each
i
,
f_{i}
is a smooth function supported in
U
such that
\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. In particular,
f_{i}
is intrinsic Lipschitz. Furthermore,
f_{i}|_{S_{i-1}^{c}}=\psi_{i}|_{S_{i-1}^{c}}
and
\mu(S_{i})\lesssim iA^{-4}
.

By (
17
), it suffices to show that
\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\mu(S_{i-1})\lesssim iA^{-4}
.

We will need some bounds from
[
NY20
]
.
As in
[
NY20
]
, let

\displaystyle D_{i}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\psi_{i+1}}\psi_{i+1}-\nabla_{\psi_{i}}\psi_{i}

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{i}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{f_{i+1}}f_{i+1}-\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}.

By (
16
) and (
17
), we have
f_{i+1}|_{S_{i}^{c}}=\psi_{i+1}|_{S_{i}^{c}}
and
f_{i+1}|_{S_{i}}=f_{i}|_{S_{i}}
. Therefore,

\tilde{D}_{i}=\mathbf{1}_{S_{i}^{c}}D_{i}
. In particular,
\|\tilde{D}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\|D_{i}\|_{\infty}
.

The following bounds on
\tilde{D}_{i}
are based on the bounds on
D_{i}
proved in
[
NY20
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
j\leq i
and
x,y\in Q_{i,k}
. Then

\displaystyle|\tilde{D}_{j}(x)-\tilde{D}_{j}(y)|\lesssim A^{-2}\rho^{j-i}.

Proof.

The
D_{j}
–version of this inequality is Lemma 3.12 of
[
NY20
]
. The proof only uses the
L_{\infty}
bounds of
D_{j}
and derivatives of
D_{j}
. As
\tilde{D}_{j}
satisfy those same bounds, the proof also works for
\tilde{D}_{j}
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

For every
\rho\geq 8
and
A\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{D}_{i}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim A^{-2},\qquad\forall i\geq 0,

\displaystyle|\langle\tilde{D}_{i},\tilde{D}_{j}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim A^{-4}\rho^{j-i},\qquad\forall 0\leq i\leq j.

(18)

Proof.

The corresponding
D_{i}
version of the inequalities is Lemma 3.9 from
[
NY20
]
. The first inequality now follows from the bound
\|\tilde{D}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\|D_{i}\|_{\infty}
. The proof of the second bound in
[
NY20
]
uses the bound

\left|\int_{Q_{n,k}}D_{m}(w)D_{n}(w)\,\mathrm{d}w\right|\lesssim A^{-4}\rho^{m-n}\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q_{n,k})

for
n\geq m
.

Let
0\leq i\leq j
. For each
1\leq k\leq k_{j}
, we consider two cases. If
Q_{j,k}\in\mathcal{S}_{j}
, then
\tilde{D}_{j}=0
on
Q_{j,k}
, so
\int_{Q_{j,k}}\tilde{D}_{i}\tilde{D}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}w=0
. Otherwise, if
Q_{j,k}\not\in\mathcal{S}_{j}
, then
\interior Q_{j,k}\cap S_{i}=\interior Q_{j,k}\cap S_{j}=\emptyset
, so
\tilde{D}_{i}=D_{i}
and
\tilde{D}_{j}=D_{j}
on
Q_{j,k}
. Therefore,

\left|\int_{Q_{j,k}}\tilde{D}_{i}(w)\tilde{D}_{j}(w)\,\mathrm{d}w\right|\lesssim A^{-4}\rho^{j-i}\mathcal{H}^{3}(Q_{j,k}).

Since the
Q_{j,k}
’s partition
U
, we sum this inequality over
k
to obtain (
18
).
∎

Now we use these bounds to show that
\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
A
is sufficiently large, then for all
i
,
\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.

Proof.

We suppose that
A
is large enough that
\|\tilde{D}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{4}
for all
i
and proceed by induction on
i
. Since
f_{0}=0
, the lemma is clear for
i=0
.

Suppose that
i\geq 0
and
\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. On one hand, if
v\not\in S_{i}
, then
|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(v)|\leq\frac{1}{2}
, and

|\nabla_{f_{i+1}}f_{i+1}(v)|\leq|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(v)|+\|\tilde{D}_{i}\|_{\infty}<1.

On the other hand, if
v\in S_{i}
, then
|\nabla_{f_{i+1}}f_{i+1}(v)|=|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(v)|\leq 1
by (
16
).
∎

It remains to bound
\mu(S_{i})
. Let
i\geq 0
. Recall that
S_{i}=\bigcup(\mathcal{S}_{i})
and that any pseudoquad
Q\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
either satisfies
\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{L_{\infty}(Q)}\geq\frac{1}{2}
or is a child of some pseudoquad of
\mathcal{S}_{i-1}
. Let

\mathcal{M}_{i}=\mathcal{S}_{i}\setminus\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{S}_{i-1}}\mathcal{C}(Q).

Then if
M\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
, then
\|\nabla_{f_{i-1}}f_{i-1}(x)\|_{L_{\infty}(M)}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. If
Q\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{i}
, then
Q
is a child of an element of
\mathcal{S}_{i-1}
. By induction, any
Q\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
is a descendant of an element of
\mathcal{S}_{j}
for some
j\leq i
, i.e.,
Q
is a descendant of an element of
\mathcal{B}_{i}:=\bigcup_{j=0}^{i}\mathcal{M}_{j}
. Furthermore, if
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
,
M\neq M^{\prime}
, then neither is a descendant of the other, so
M
and
M^{\prime}
are disjoint.

We will thus bound
\mu(S_{i})
by bounding the size of
\mathcal{B}_{i}
, then bounding the size of the set of descendants of pseudoquads in
\mathcal{B}_{i}
. We bound
\mathcal{B}_{i}
by showing that
\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}
is large on the pseudoquads in
\mathcal{B}_{i}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
\rho
is sufficiently large.
Let
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Then
|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(v)|\geq\frac{1}{4}
for all
v\in Q
.

Proof.

If
Q\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
, then
Q\in\mathcal{M}_{j}
for some
j\leq i
, so
\|\nabla_{f_{j}}f_{j}\|_{L_{\infty}(Q)}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Furthermore, since
Q\subset S_{j}
, (
16
) implies that
\nabla_{f_{j}}f_{j}=\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}
on
Q
.

Let
y\in Q
be such that
|\nabla_{f_{j}}f_{j}(y)|\geq 1/2
, and let
x\in Q
. By Lemma
3.2
,

|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(x)-\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(y)|=|\nabla_{f_{j}}f_{j}(x)-\nabla_{f_{j}}f_{j}(y)|\\
\leq\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}|\tilde{D}_{k}(x)-\tilde{D}_{k}(y)|\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}A^{-2}\rho^{k-j}\leq 2A^{-2}\rho^{-1}.

If
\rho
is sufficiently large, this gives
|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(x)-\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(y)|
and thus
|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(x)|\geq\frac{1}{4}
, as desired.
∎

Thus, we can bound the size of
\mathcal{M}_{k}
using the following bound on
\nabla_{f_{k}}f_{k}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For all
k
,

\|\nabla_{f_{k}}f_{k}\|_{2}\lesssim A^{-2}\sqrt{k}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.3
,

\int_{V_{0}}|\nabla_{f_{k}}f_{k}(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{V_{0}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\tilde{D}_{j}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\|\tilde{D}_{j}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+2\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq k-1}\langle\tilde{D}_{i},\tilde{D}_{j}\rangle\lesssim kA^{-4}+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}A^{-4}\rho^{-k}\lesssim kA^{-4},

so
\|\nabla_{f_{k}}f_{k}\|_{2}\lesssim A^{-2}\sqrt{k}
.
∎

Now we bound the size of the set of descendants of a pseudoquad.
We will need the following lemma, which is part of Lemma 3.10 of
[
NY20
]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
R_{z}
be the
z
-coordinate of any of the maps
R_{i,j}
. If
\rho>8
, then for all
(s,t)\in[0,Ar_{i}]\times[0,r_{i}^{2}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}\leq\frac{\partial R_{z}}{\partial t}(s,t)\leq\frac{4}{3}.

The following bound on the heights of pseudoquads follows immediately.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
i\geq 0
and let
1\leq j\leq k_{i}
. Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
g_{1},g_{2}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
be such that

Q_{i,j}=\{(x,0,z)\mid x\in I,z\in[g_{1}(x),g_{2}(x)]\}.

Then

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}r_{i}^{2}\leq g_{2}(x)-g_{1}(x)\leq\frac{4}{3}r_{i}^{2},\qquad\forall x\in I.

(19)

Proof.

Let
x_{0}=\min(I)
. Then in fact,
Q_{i,j}
is the image of a map
R_{i,j}\colon[0,Ar_{i}]\times[0,r_{i}^{2}]\to V_{0}
such that
R_{i,j}(s,t)=(x_{0}+s,0,R_{z}(s,t))
. In particular,
g_{1}(x)=R_{z}(x-x_{0},0)
and
g_{2}(x)=R_{z}(x-x_{0},r_{i}^{2})
. The Mean Value Theorem along with Lemma
3.7
then gives the desired bound.
∎

As each
Q_{i,j}
has width
Ar_{i}
, we immediately get the following corollary.

Corollary 3.9
.

For any
i,j\geq 0
, we have that
\frac{3}{4}Ar_{i}^{3}\leq|Q_{i,j}|\leq\frac{4}{3}Ar_{i}^{3}
.

For each pseudoquad
Q
, let
\tilde{Q}=\bigcup(\mathcal{D}(Q))
so that

S_{i}\subset\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\tilde{Q}.

(20)

Our next lemma bounds
\mu(\tilde{Q})
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For any
i
and any
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
,
\mu(\tilde{Q})\lesssim\mu(Q)
.

Proof.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
g_{1},g_{2}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
be such that
I
is an interval of length
Ar_{i}
and

Q=\left\{(x,0,z)\in V_{0}\mid x\in I,z\in[g_{1}(x),g_{2}(x)]\right\}.

We consider
\bigcup(\mathcal{C}(Q))
. If
Q^{\prime}
is a child of
Q
, then

Q^{\prime}=\left\{(x,0,z)\in V_{0}\mid x\in I^{\prime},z\in[g^{\prime}_{1}(x),g^{\prime}_{2}(x)]\right\}

for some
I^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime}
, and
g_{2}^{\prime}
such that
I^{\prime}\subset I
.

By our choice of
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
, the top and bottom curves of
Q
are characteristic curves of
\psi_{i}
and the top and bottom curves of
Q^{\prime}
are characteristic curves of
\psi_{i+1}
. Since
\kappa_{i}
is 0 on a neighborhood of
\partial Q
, we have
\psi_{i}=\psi_{i+1}
on
\partial Q
, so the top and bottom curves of
Q
are also characteristic curves of
\psi_{i+1}
. Since
\psi_{i+1}
is smooth, its characteristic curves don’t intersect, so the top and bottom edges of
Q^{\prime}
don’t cross
\partial Q
. Thus, since there is some
x\in I^{\prime}
such that
[g_{1}(x),g_{2}(x)]
intersects
[g_{1}^{\prime}(x),g_{2}^{\prime}(x)]
, it must be true that
[g_{1}(x),g_{2}(x)]
intersects
[g_{1}^{\prime}(x),g_{2}^{\prime}(x)]
for all
x\in I^{\prime}
. By Lemma
3.8
, this implies

[g_{1}^{\prime}(x),g_{2}^{\prime}(x)]\subset[g_{1}(x)-\frac{4}{3}r_{i+1}^{2},g_{2}(x)+\frac{4}{3}r_{i+1}^{2}]
and thus

\bigcup(\mathcal{C}(Q))\subset\left\{(x,0,z)\in V_{0}:x\in I,z\in\left[g_{1}(x)-\frac{4}{3}r_{i+1}^{2},g_{2}(x)+\frac{4}{3}r_{i+1}^{2}\right]\right\}.

By induction,

\tilde{Q}\subset\left\{(x,0,z)\in V_{0}:x\in I,z\in\left[g_{1}(x)-\sum_{j=i}^{\infty}\frac{4}{3}r_{j+1}^{2},g_{2}(x)+\sum_{j=i}^{\infty}\frac{4}{3}r_{j+1}^{2}\right]\right\}.

The upper and lower bounds are geometric series, so by Corollary
3.9
,

\mu(\tilde{Q})\leq\mu(Q)+\frac{16}{3}r_{i+1}^{2}\cdot Ar_{i}\leq\mu(Q)+\frac{16}{3}\rho^{-2}Ar_{i}^{3}\leq 4\mu(Q).

∎

Finally, we prove the proposition.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Let
i\geq 0
. By Lemma
3.4
, we have
\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. It remains to bound the measure of
S_{i}
.

By (
20
), we have

S_{i}\subset\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\tilde{Q},
where
\mathcal{B}_{i}
is a collection of disjoint pseudoquads. Furthermore, by Lemma
3.5
, we have
|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}(v)|\geq\frac{1}{4}
for all
v\in\bigcup(\mathcal{B}_{i})
. By Lemma
3.10
,

\mu(S_{i})\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mu(\tilde{Q})\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{B}_{i}}\mu(Q)=\mu\left(\bigcup(\mathcal{B}_{i})\right).

By Chebyshev’s Inequality and Lemma
3.6
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(\bigcup(\mathcal{B}_{i})\right)\leq 16\|\nabla_{f_{i}}f_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim iA^{-4},

so
\mu(S_{i})\lesssim iA^{-4}
, as desired.
∎

In addition to the intrinsic Lipschitz condition,
f_{i}
satisfies a higher-order Sobolev condition. We state this condition in terms of a family of differential operators on smooth functions
V_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
Z
be the operator
Z=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
. The pseudoquads in
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
have width
Ar_{i}
and height
r_{i}^{2}
, and we define rescaled operators

\displaystyle\hat{Z}_{i}

\displaystyle=r_{i}^{2}Z

\displaystyle\hat{\partial}_{i}

\displaystyle=Ar_{i}\nabla_{f_{i}}.

For
i\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, we let
\{\hat{Z}_{i},\hat{\partial}_{i}\}^{n}
denote the differential operators
E
that can be expressed as
E=E_{1}\cdots E_{n}

where
E_{j}\in\{\hat{Z}_{i},\hat{\partial}_{i}\}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
. We call these
words of length
n
in the alphabet
\{\hat{Z}_{i},\hat{\partial}_{i}\}
. As a special case,
\{\hat{Z}_{i},\hat{\partial}_{i}\}^{0}=\{\id\}
.

The following lemma bounds
Ef_{i}
when
E\in\{\hat{Z}_{i},\hat{\partial}_{i}\}^{k}
. This generalizes the bounds in
[
NY20
, Lemma 3.10]
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Given
d\geq 2
, there exists
\rho_{0}>0
so that if
\rho\geq\rho_{0}
,
i\geq 0
,
k\leq d
, and
E\in\{\hat{Z}_{i},\hat{\partial}_{i}\}^{k}
, then

\displaystyle\|E\nu_{i}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{d}A^{-1}r_{i}.

Furthermore, if
E\not\in\{\id,\hat{\partial}_{i}\}
, then

\displaystyle\|Ef_{i}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{d}A^{-1}r_{i}\rho^{-1}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{f_{i}}^{2}f_{i}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim A^{-3}r_{i}^{-1}\rho^{-1}

\displaystyle\|Zf_{i}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim A^{-1}r_{i}^{-1}\rho^{-1}.

(21)

The coefficients in this lemma are related to the dimensions of the pseudoquads in
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
. As noted above, these pseudoquads have width and height corresponding to
\hat{\partial}_{i}
and
\hat{Z}_{i}
. The coefficient
A^{-1}r_{i}
comes from the fact that
\|\nu_{i}\|_{\infty}=A^{-1}r_{i}\|\kappa\|_{\infty}\approx A^{-1}r_{i}
. Thus, when
\rho
is large,
f_{i}
is close to affine on any of the pseudoquads in
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.

The proof of Lemma
3.11
is rather technical, and we leave it to Appendix
A
.

3.1.
Rescaling

Let
\Sigma_{i}=\Gamma_{f_{i}}
. Because the singular integrals we consider are scale-invariant and translation-invariant, it will be convenient to define rescaled and translated versions of
f_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
. Let
i\geq 0
and let
p_{0}\in\Sigma_{i}
.

Let
s_{i}:=s_{r_{i}^{-1}}
. Let
\alpha=\alpha_{p_{0},i}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\alpha(p)=r_{i}^{-1}\left(-y(p_{0})+f_{i}(p_{0}s^{-1}_{i}(p))\right).

By Lemmas
2.2
and
2.3
, we have
\Gamma_{\alpha}=s_{i}(p_{0}^{-1}\Sigma_{i})
and

\Psi_{\alpha}(p)=s_{i}\left(p_{0}^{-1}\Psi_{f_{i}}(p_{0}s^{-1}_{i}(p))\right).

In particular, we have
\mathbf{0}\in\Gamma_{\alpha}
and
\alpha(\mathbf{0})=0
.

Let
\gamma=\gamma_{p_{0},i}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\gamma(p)=r_{i}^{-1}\nu_{i}(p_{0}s^{-1}_{i}(p)).

Then for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\alpha(p)+t\gamma(p)=r_{i}^{-1}(-y(p_{0})+(f_{i}+t\nu_{i})(p_{0}s^{-1}_{i}(p)))

and

\Gamma_{\alpha+t\gamma}=s_{i}(p_{0}^{-1}\Gamma_{f_{i}+t\nu_{i}}).

These functions satisfy the following consequence of Lemma
3.11
.

Lemma 3.12
.

There exists
\rho_{0}>0
and
c>0
such that if
\rho\geq\rho_{0}
,
i\geq 0
,
k\leq 3
, and
\alpha
and
\gamma
are defined as above for some
p_{0}\in\Sigma_{i}
, then
\|\gamma\|_{\infty}\leq cA^{-1}
,
\|\nabla_{\alpha}\alpha\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, and

\|F\gamma\|_{\infty}\leq cA^{-\#\nabla_{\alpha}(F)-1},\qquad\forall F\in\{\nabla_{\alpha},Z\}^{k},

(22)

where
\#\nabla_{\alpha}(F)
is the number of occurrences of
\nabla_{\alpha}
in
F
.
Moreover, if
F\notin\{\id,\nabla_{\alpha}\}
, then

\|F\alpha\|_{\infty}\leq cA^{-\#\nabla_{\alpha}(F)-1}\rho^{-1}.

(23)

Proof.

In fact, we will show that for any
d\geq 2
, there is a
\rho_{0}
such that if
\rho\geq\rho_{0}
,
i\geq 0
, then (
22
) and (
23
) hold for all
k\leq d
.
Let
\hat{\partial}=A\nabla_{\alpha}
. It suffices to show that if
\rho
is sufficiently large, then
\|F\gamma\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{d}A^{-1}
for all
F\in\{\hat{\partial},Z\}^{k}
and, if
F\notin\{\id,\hat{\partial}\}
,
\|F\alpha\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{d}\rho^{-1}
.

Let
r=r_{i}
and
s=s_{r^{-1}}
. Let
L(g)=s(p_{0}^{-1}g)
so that
\Gamma_{\alpha}=L(\Sigma_{i})

and

\displaystyle\alpha(p)

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\left(-y(p_{0})+(f_{i}\circ L^{-1})(p)\right),

\displaystyle\gamma(p)

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\nu_{i}\circ L^{-1}(p).

Since
L
sends horizontal curves in
\Sigma_{i}
to horizontal curves in
\Gamma_{\alpha}
, it sends integral curves of
\nabla_{f_{i}}
to integral curves of
\nabla_{\alpha}
. Therefore,
L_{*}\nabla_{f_{i}}=r^{-1}\nabla_{\alpha}
, and

\displaystyle L_{*}\hat{\partial}_{i}=L_{*}(Ar\nabla_{f_{i}})=Arr^{-1}\nabla_{\alpha}=\hat{\partial}.

(24)

Likewise,
L_{*}\hat{Z}_{i}=L_{*}(r^{2}Z)=Z
.

Let
F=F(\hat{\partial},Z)
be a word of length at most
d
in the letters
\hat{\partial}
and
Z
and let
F^{\prime}=F(\hat{\partial}_{i},\hat{Z}_{i})
be
F
with
\hat{\partial}
replaced by
\hat{\partial}_{i}
and
Z
by
\hat{Z}_{i}
. Then
L_{*}(F^{\prime})=F
, so

F\gamma=F[r^{-1}(\nu_{i}\circ L^{-1})]=r^{-1}F^{\prime}[\nu_{i}]\circ L^{-1}.

Lemma
3.11
implies that if
\rho
is sufficiently large, then
\|F\gamma\|_{\infty}=r^{-1}\|F^{\prime}[\nu_{i}]\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{d}A^{-1}
. This proves (
22
).

Similarly, if
